# Verification ledger

Claims verified by hand because no script can reach their source. `scripts/lint-claims.cjs` reads this file and reports a matching REVIEW as LEDGERED. Every line needs a reason code, how it was verified and the date; re-check lines older than six months.

Reason codes: BOT_WALL, LENDING_ONLY, PDF, PAYWALL, OCR_FUZZY, JS_RENDERED, DERIVED, PAGINATED (the claim is on a later page of a paginated source that the link opens at its first page).

Format, one section per entry and one line per claim (the template below is indented, so the parser ignores it):

    ## <slug>
    - "<claim text as in the entry>" | <cited URL as linked> | <CODE> | <how verified> | <YYYY-MM-DD>

Real sections start with `## the-slug` at the start of a line, followed by one `- ` line per claim.

## when-was-jesus-actually-born
- "a Roman almanac made 171 years before the Easter tables" | https://www.tertullian.org/fathers/chronography_of_354_00_eintro.htm | DERIVED | 525 - 354 = 171: the cited Tertullian Project introduction says Valentinus 'received a codex containing an illustrated calendar for the year 354'; the Easter-table year 525 is Dionysius's own worked example ('fiunt simul anni DXXV', henk-reints.nl/cal/audette/denys.html); the gate's 'ok' on 171 is only the digits appearing in the consul list; both pages read in full | 2026-10-30
- "15 times 34, plus 12" | https://henk-reints.nl/cal/audette/denys.html | DERIVED | Argumentum primum of the Liber de Paschate (Latin, Audette transcription) reads 'computa quindecies XXXIV, fiunt DX; iis semper adde XII regulares, fiunt DXXII; adde etiam indictionem ... tertiam, consulatu Probi junioris, fiunt simul anni DXXV'; Roman numerals converted (15 x 34 = 510, +12 = 522, +3 = 525); page read in full; the gate passes 15, 34 and 12 only because those digits happen to appear on the NASA calendars page | 2026-10-30
- "the total in Dionysius's worked example was 525." | https://henk-reints.nl/cal/audette/denys.html | DERIVED | Audette transcription, Argumentum primum: 'ut puta, tertiam, consulatu Probi junioris, fiunt simul anni DXXV. Isti sunt anni ab incarnatione Domini'; DXXV = 525; page read in full | 2026-10-30
- "the AD count came five centuries later, in 525," | https://henk-reints.nl/cal/audette/denys.html | DERIVED | Audette transcription of Dionysius's Liber de Paschate: the preface says 'elegimus ab incarnatione Domini nostri Jesu Christi annorum tempora praenotare', and Argumentum II gives the current year as 'annos ab incarnatione Domini nostri Jesu Christi DXXV' (consulship of Probus junior); DXXV = 525; page read in full | 2026-10-30
- "much less does he explain or justify the underlying date." | https://archive.org/details/oxfordcompaniont00blac | LENDING_ONLY | archive.org full-text search API (be-api.us.archive.org/fts/v1/search) scoped to identifier:oxfordcompaniont00blac (Blackburn and Holford-Strevens, The Oxford Companion to the Year, OUP 1999), scan page 986: 'nowhere in his exposition of his table does Dionysius relate his epoch to any other dating system, whether consulate, Olympiad, year of the world, or regnal year of Augustus; much less does he explain or justify the underlying date, or even claim it for his own discovery'; the entry's paraphrase is about the epoch (the starting point), as the book is | 2026-10-30

- "the ratio of the mental age (as reported on a standardized test) to the chronological age multiplied by 100" | https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/IQ | BOT_WALL | HTTP 403 to the gate; read with curl and a browser user agent (HTTP 200), entry IQ, noun sense 1b, verbatim, followed by sense 2 'proficiency in or knowledge of a specified subject' | 2026-10-25
- "the number resulting from the division of one number by another," | https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/quotient | BOT_WALL | HTTP 403 to the gate; read with curl and a browser user agent (HTTP 200): sense 1 'the number resulting from the division of one number by another'; etymology 'Latin quotiens how many times, from quot how many' | 2026-10-25
- "1913" | https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/intelligence%20quotient | BOT_WALL | HTTP 403 to the gate and to WebFetch; curl with a browser user agent got HTTP 200, page text: 'First Known Use 1913, in the meaning defined above' | 2026-10-25
- "1915" | https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/IQ | BOT_WALL | HTTP 403 to the gate; read with curl and a browser user agent (HTTP 200): 'First Known Use Noun 1915, in the meaning defined at sense 1' | 2026-10-25
- "a standard score metric with a mean of 100 and an SD of 15," | https://www.pearsonassessments.com/content/dam/school/global/clinical/us/assets/wisc-v/wisc-v-interpretive-report.pdf | PDF | Pearson 'WISC-V Interpretive Considerations for Sample Report (10/20/2020)', downloaded with curl and read with pdftotext, About WISC-V Scores, verbatim: 'The primary index scores and the FSIQ are on a standard score metric with a mean of 100 and an SD of 15. ## is-canola-oil-banned-in-europe
- "at 20 grams per kilogram, or 2 percent" | https://publications.europa.eu/resource/celex/32023R0915 | DERIVED | Regulation (EU) 2023/915 Annex I section 2 Plant toxins, entry 2.1.1, gives erucic acid in vegetable oils and fats as 20,0 g/kg (read through the EU Publications Office Cellar URL with browser headers); 20 g/kg is 2 percent by weight; the claim gate matches 20 and 2 percent only through unrelated rows on that page | 2026-10-26
- "Canola oil is rapeseed oil from varieties that meet a standard first reached by Canadian breeders: less than 2 percent erucic acid in the oil, with low glucosinolates in the seed meal." | https://www.canolacouncil.org/about-canola/ | DERIVED | FAQ answer, which the claim gate does not reach. The About Canola page gives the standard ('less than 2% erucic acid in its fatty acid profile', under 30 micromoles of glucosinolates per gram of meal) and names no country; the Council's history page (https://www.canolacouncil.org/canola-encyclopedia/history-of-canola-seed-development/) says Stefansson developed Tower in 1974, 'the first low erucic acid, low glucosinolate cultivar', so 'first reached by Canadian breeders' is derived from the two pages | 2026-10-26
- "Only two countries produced more rapeseed oil than Germany in 2023" | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapeseed_oil | DERIVED | fact-checker's read of FAOSTAT bulk file Production_Crops_Livestock_E_All_Data.zip (release 2025-12-23), item 271 'Rapeseed or canola oil, crude', element 5510, Y2023: Canada 4,422,368 t (A), China mainland 4,326,300 t (X, unofficial), Germany 4,181,589 t (A), India 4,113,000 t (A), World 31,399,693 t; matches the Wikipedia table | 2026-10-30
- "trademarked these "double low" rapeseeds as canola in 1978" | https://science.nasa.gov/earth/earth-observatory/canadian-canola-fields-145517 | PDF | NASA page re-read 2026-10-30: 'In 1978, the Western Canadian Oilseed Crushers trademarked these "double low" rapeseeds as canola'; corroborated by Rapeseed Digest Dec 1978 p.3 (http://canolahistory.ca/wp-content/uploads/2017/02/1978-Rapeseed-Digest-vol12-Canola-name-2.pdf, scan with no text layer, read as an image by the fact-checker): 'the Western Canadian Crushers Association have registered the name "Canola" as a certified mark'; Purdue AFCM says 1979, which this contemporary record contradicts; do not 'correct' to 1979 | 2026-10-30
- "the Canola Council of Canada says many cultivars of winter rapeseed, a type grown in Europe and Asia, fit the canola definition." | https://www.canolacouncil.org/canola-encyclopedia/history-of-canola-seed-development/ | DERIVED | FAQ answer, which the claim gate does not reach; the history page says winter rapeseed is 'widely grown in parts of Europe and Asia' and that many of its cultivars have low erucic acid and glucosinolate levels 'and fit the canola definition' (page read 2026-10-30) | 2026-10-30

## what-are-irish-twins
- "1852" | https://archive.org/details/janegreyswisshel00hoff | LENDING_ONLY | archive.org full-text search API (be-api.us.archive.org/fts/v1/search, query ANDed with identifier:janegreyswisshel00hoff; Sylvia D. If the full moon falls on a Sunday, Easter Day is the Sunday following. - "Its earliest Easters are March 23, in 1913 and 2008." | https://www.bcponline.org/Misc/tables.html | DERIVED | parsed 'A Table to Find Easter Day' (1900-2089) on the page: no March 22 in the Easter column, earliest rows 1913 March 23 and 2008* March 23, April 25 only in 1943 and 2038; re-checked by a fresh download on 2026-10-30 and cross-checked with the Gauss/Meeus Gregorian algorithm by two separate agents | 2026-10-30

## how-are-tvs-measured
- "For 52 and a half years" | https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-1966-03-03/pdf/FR-1966-03-03.pdf#page=13 | DERIVED | rule effective July 1, 1966 (31 FR 3343, govinfo PDF p. 13, pdftotext: 'This rule becomes effective on July 1, 1966.'); repeal effective January 7, 2019 (83 FR 50484, govinfo HTML 2018-21803: 'This rule is effective January 7, 2019.'); interval 52 years, 6 months, 6 days | 2026-10-30
- "The Federal Trade Commission's rule took effect on July 1, 1966, and a seller who quoted any other dimension had to name it 'clearly and conspicuously' beside the number." | https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-1966-03-03/pdf/FR-1966-03-03.pdf#page=13 | PDF | Federal Register vol. 31 no. 42, March 3, 1966, 16 CFR Part 410, page 3343 (govinfo PDF p. 13), pdftotext: 'This rule becomes effective on July 1, 1966.' (adopted February 24, 1966); sec. 410.3(b) requires a size other than the horizontal to carry 'a statement, in close connection and conjunction therewith, clearly and conspicuously showing the manner of measurement' | 2026-10-30
- "In March 1950, Popular Science announced a change of shape" | https://www.earlytelevision.org/pdf/pop_sci_3-50-1.pdf | PDF | one-page image-only PDF (no text layer), read visually: article 'Rectangular TV Tube Cuts Cost'; the 'Popular Science, March 1950' caption was added by the host, so the date was confirmed in the full March 1950 issue (archive.org bub_gb_KS0DAAAAMBAJ, contents line 'Rectangular TV Tube Cuts Cost 123', p. 123, same wording) | 2026-10-30
- "After years of trying to squeeze a rectangular television picture into a round tube, TV manufacturers have come up with the obvious solution - a rectangular tube." | https://www.earlytelevision.org/pdf/pop_sci_3-50-1.pdf | PDF | image-only PDF read visually (Popular Science, March 1950, p. 123): 'AFTER years of trying to squeeze a rectangular television picture into a round tube, TV manufacturers have come up with the obvious solution--a rectangular tube.' (the double hyphen rendered as the house dash) | 2026-10-30
- "On January 1, 1949, the trade newsletter Television Digest described Garod's new Tele-Zoom, which had a 'round screen, just like Zenith's,' and a switch that gave the viewer a 'choice of either circular or rectangular picture.'" | https://worldradiohistory.com/Archive-TV-Digest/40s/TV-Digest-1949-01.pdf#page=14 | PDF | January 1949 compilation, PDF p. 14 (pdftotext and scan), Vol. 5 No. 1 dated January 1, 1949, item 'GAROD GIMMICK & OTHER TRADE NOTES': 'Garod's new "Tele-Zoom" TV receiver ... has round screen, just like Zenith's, but viewer has choice of either circular or rectangular picture by flipping switch at end of cord' | 2026-10-30
- "It came in 10-, 12- and 15-inch models" | https://worldradiohistory.com/Archive-TV-Digest/40s/TV-Digest-1949-01.pdf#page=14 | PDF | same item, PDF p. 14, Vol. 5 No. 1, January 1, 1949: 'Models are 10, 12, 15-in., priced same as before (see TV Directory No. 6); deliveries begin Jan. 15.' | 2026-10-30
- "In August 1949, Television Digest reported growing sentiment in the trade for dropping square-inch claims and 'to adhere henceforth strictly to diameter designations,' and two weeks later that RCA and Motorola had agreed 'to stick to tube diameters in future ads.'" | https://worldradiohistory.com/Archive-TV-Digest/40s/TV-Digest-1949-08.pdf#page=27 | PDF | August 1949 compilation (pdftotext and scan); PDF p. 27, Vol. 5 No. 33, August 13, 1949: 'There's growing sentiment for discarding "sq. in." claims for picture tube surfaces, and to adhere henceforth strictly to diameter designations'; PDF p. 42, Vol. 5 No. 35, August 27, 1949: 'Move to abolish references to "sq. in." picture size (Vol. 5:33) is gaining momentum: RCA and Motorola have agreed to stick to tube diameters in future ads' | 2026-10-30
- "It was industry practice, the Commission wrote, 'to mask or cover the walls of such tube and a minimal amount of the actual picture area thereof,' so a size given as '19 inch set - overall diagonal measure' was 'invariably larger' than the picture." | https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-1966-03-03/pdf/FR-1966-03-03.pdf#page=12 | PDF | 31 FR 3342 (govinfo PDF p. 12), pdftotext: sec. 410.2(a) 'It is a practice in the industry, when installing a picture tube in a television receiving set, to mask or cover the walls of such tube and a minimal amount of the actual picture area thereof ... Thus, the overall dimensions are invariably larger than the dimensions of the picture shown.'; sec. 410.1 '"19 inch set-- overall diagonal measure."' (the double hyphen rendered as the house dash) | 2026-10-30
- "FTC staff had tried a softer fix in 1956" | https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-1966-03-03/pdf/FR-1966-03-03.pdf#page=12 | PDF | 31 FR 3342, sec. 410.2(c): 'In 1956, during the course of a compliance program initiated under the Trade Practice Rules for the Radio and Television Industry ... the industry was advised that the staff would not object to size representations in terms of overall diagonal measurements of picture tubes provided disclosure also was made of the sizes of the actual picture areas and additional disclosure was made of the fact that such dimensions were overall and diagonal when so measured.' | 2026-10-30
- "But the record was 'replete with television advertisements wherein the overall sizes of the advertised sets are emphasized in large figures or print,' and wherever the picture size appeared it was 'either inadequate or so inconspicuous as to be of no value in removing or curing the inherent deceptive tendency.'" | https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-1966-03-03/pdf/FR-1966-03-03.pdf#page=12 | PDF | 31 FR 3342, sec. 410.2(c), pdftotext: 'the record of this proceeding is replete with television advertisements wherein the overall sizes of the advertised sets are emphasized in large figures or print. - "A full 19-by-15 rectangle measures about 24.2 inches corner to corner and covers 285 square inches" | https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-1994-11-02/html/94-27012.htm | DERIVED | sqrt(19^2 + 15^2) = sqrt(586) = 24.21; 19 x 15 = 285; the cited page gives the rule's example screen as 'measuring 19 inches horizontally, 15 inches vertically, and 20 inches diagonally' with 'a picture area of 262 square inches', both less than a full rectangle gives; a picture with corners rounded to a radius of about 5.2 in has both (fact-checker's recomputation) | 2026-10-30
- "Blankets, it noted, were sized as '76 x 107 inches' and rugs as '9 ft. x 12 ft.,' so, it concluded, shoppers would take a '19 inch set' to mean a picture 19 inches across." | https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-1966-03-03/pdf/FR-1966-03-03.pdf#page=13 | PDF | 31 FR 3342-43, sec. 410.2(d): 'The sizes of blankets, for example, are invariably stated in terms of the width and length, e.g., "76 x 107 inches." (blankets on PDF p. 12, rugs on p. 13); sec. 410.2(e), p. 13: 'representations in terms of diagonal dimensions of rectangular picture tubes, e.g., "19 inch set," are misleading because consumers would believe that television sets so described would show a picture measured 19 inches horizontally unless adequate explanation is given to the contrary' | 2026-10-30
- "Except in the matter under consideration, the Commission is not aware of any rectangular shaped object the size of which is stated in terms of the diagonal measurement." | https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-1966-03-03/pdf/FR-1966-03-03.pdf#page=13 | PDF | 31 FR 3343 (govinfo PDF p. 13), sec. 410.2(d), verbatim, following the rugs example | 2026-10-30
- "since 16 divided by 9 is about 1.78" | https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2006-06-14/html/E6-9233.htm | DERIVED | 16 / 9 = 1.778; the cited page's footnote 1 reads 'Traditional televisions have an aspect ratio of 4 by 3 (1.33 to 1) while wider screen high definition televisions have an aspect ratio of 16 by 9 (1.85 to 1)'; the FTC's 2017 notice (2017-13476) gives '16:9 (1.77:1)' | 2026-10-30
- "At 65 inches, the wider shape gains 4.7 inches of width but loses 7.1 inches of height, which costs about 11 percent of the area." | https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2006-06-14/html/E6-9233.htm | DERIVED | 65-inch 4:3 is 52.0 x 39.0 in (2,028 sq in); 65-inch 16:9 is 65 x 16/sqrt(337) = 56.65 by 65 x 9/sqrt(337) = 31.87 in (1,805 sq in); width +4.65, height -7.13, area ratio 1,805 / 2,028 = 0.890; the two ratios are on the cited page | 2026-10-30

## how-does-a-compass-work
- "about 24 degrees" | https://www.ngdc.noaa.gov/geomag/calculators/magcalc.shtml | DERIVED | the linked calculator is an input form; its declination API (ngdc.noaa.gov/geomag-web/calculators/calculateDeclination, model=IGRF, Greenwich lat 51.477 lon 0, 1 July each year, read with curl) answers for these dates with model GUFM1810 to GUFM1825 (gufm1, Jackson, Jonkers and Walker): 1810 -24.19, 1815 -24.38, 1816 -24.39, 1817 -24.38, 1820 -24.31, 1825 -24.09 degrees, so the westward maximum is about 24 degrees | 2026-10-30
- "around 1820" | https://www.ngdc.noaa.gov/geomag/calculators/magcalc.shtml | DERIVED | same API, model GUFM1820 (gufm1): -24.31 degrees for 1820-07-01, within 0.1 degree of the 1816-1817 peak (-24.39); the last zero declination at Greenwich falls in mid-1663 (+0.47 in 1660, +0.005 in 1663, -0.32 in 1665), consistent with BGS 'since around 1660' and about 356 years before September 2019, so the entry says 'about 360 years' | 2026-10-30
- "puts the westward swing at its widest" | https://www.ngdc.noaa.gov/geomag/calculators/magcalc.shtml | DERIVED | same API series (gufm1), Greenwich: 1800 -23.61, 1810 -24.19, 1816 -24.39, 1820 -24.31, 1830 -23.82 degrees: the westward declination peaks in 1816-1817 and falls on either side; the sentence names the model and says NOAA's calculator uses it, not that NOAA made it | 2026-10-30
- "is able to point to the south. - "a single cocoon fibre of new silk." | https://archive.org/details/sciencecivilisat0004need | LENDING_ONLY | full-text search scoped to this item: 'It is best to suspend it by a single cocoon fibre of new silk attached to the centre of the needle' | 2026-10-30

## where-do-capers-come-from
- "only the berries are tithed since they [alone] count as fruit," | https://www.sefaria.org/Mishnah_Maasrot.4.6 | JS_RENDERED | the Sefaria page renders its text with JavaScript (the gate reads 520 characters); read via www.sefaria.org/api/v3/texts/Mishnah_Maasrot.4.6, English version 'Mishnah Yomit by Dr. Joshua Kulp', word for word: "Rabbi Eliezer says: as for the caper bush, tithes must be given from the shoots, the berries and the blossoms. Rabbi Akiba says: only the berries are tithed since they [alone] count as fruit."; the Hebrew (Torat Emet 357) reads temarot, va'aviyonot ve-kafras (Bartenura and the Talmud spell the last kafrisin); Kulp's separate English Explanation loosely calls the berries 'the capers', but his translation and the commentators' reading (Bartenura: aviyonot 'is the main fruit'; Rambam: aviyonot is the ripe fruit) both leave the buds outside Akiba's 'fruit' | 2026-10-30

## what-is-an-irish-goodbye
- "The phrase is American slang that barely appears in books before 2013." | https://books.google.com/ngrams/graph?content=Irish+goodbye%2CFrench+leave&year_start=1800&year_end=2022&corpus=en&smoothing=0 | JS_RENDERED | the gate never fetches Google pages and the graph is drawn by JavaScript; read the same series from the Ngram JSON endpoint (books.google.com/ngrams/json, same query, corpus en, smoothing 0): Irish goodbye is non-zero before 2013 only in 1956, 1973, 1982, 1986, 2003, 2009, 2011 and 2012 (the largest 1.70e-10 in 1956, the rest 3.7e-11 to 6.4e-11), then 4.47e-10 in 2013 | 2026-10-30
- "Google's Ngram Viewer, which charts how often a phrase appears in scanned books, puts Irish goodbye at zero in every year before 2013 apart from a handful of stray hits." | https://books.google.com/ngrams/graph?content=Irish+goodbye%2CFrench+leave&year_start=1800&year_end=2022&corpus=en&smoothing=0 | JS_RENDERED | Ngram JSON endpoint, same query, corpus en: non-zero before 2013 only in 1956, 1973, 1982, 1986, 2003, 2009, 2011 and 2012 (8 stray years, the largest 1.70e-10 in 1956) | 2026-10-30
- "It jumps that year and roughly quadruples by 2022." | https://books.google.com/ngrams/graph?content=Irish+goodbye%2CFrench+leave&year_start=1800&year_end=2022&corpus=en&smoothing=0 | JS_RENDERED | Ngram JSON endpoint, same query: Irish goodbye 3.7e-11 in 2012, 4.47e-10 in 2013, 1.84e-09 in 2022; 1.84e-09 / 4.47e-10 = 4.1 | 2026-10-30
- "Yet in every year from 2013 to 2022, French leave turns up at least eight times as often." | https://books.google.com/ngrams/graph?content=Irish+goodbye%2CFrench+leave&year_start=1800&year_end=2022&corpus=en&smoothing=0 | JS_RENDERED | Ngram JSON endpoint, same query, corpus en: French leave / Irish goodbye ratio by year 2013-2022 = 25.9, 36.5, 76.0, 57.8, 19.2, 13.5, 9.7, 9.9, 8.5, 12.6 (lowest 8.48 in 2021; in 2022 French leave 2.31e-08 against Irish goodbye 1.84e-09) | 2026-10-30
- "It appears in American books every year from 2012, but in British books only in scattered years until 2017." | https://books.google.com/ngrams/graph?content=Irish+goodbye&year_start=1990&year_end=2022&corpus=en-US&smoothing=0 | JS_RENDERED | Ngram JSON endpoint, corpus en-US, 1990-2022: non-zero in 2003, 2009 and every year 2012-2022; corpus en-GB (the British link): non-zero in 2011, 2014 and every year 2017-2022 | 2026-10-30
- "barely in print before 2013" | https://books.google.com/ngrams/graph?content=Irish+goodbye%2CFrench+leave&year_start=1800&year_end=2022&corpus=en&smoothing=0 | JS_RENDERED | closing restatement of the Ngram series read from the JSON endpoint (corpus en): stray non-zero years only before 2013, then a jump to 4.47e-10 in 2013 | 2026-10-30
